\section{Heuristic Algorithms and Local
Search}\label{heuristic-algorithms-and-local-search}

Sometimes, a project is so complex---like scheduling 5,000 exams for
20,000 students in 500 rooms---that finding the \emph{perfect}
mathematical solution would take a supercomputer 100 years. These are
called \textbf{NP-Hard} problems. In these cases, we use
\textbf{Heuristics}. A heuristic is a fancy word for a ``smart rule of
thumb.'' It doesn't guarantee the \emph{perfect} solution, but it
guarantees a \emph{good} solution quickly.

\subsection{Theoretical Foundation}\label{theoretical-foundation-7}

\subsubsection{What are Heuristic
Algorithms?}\label{what-are-heuristic-algorithms}

Heuristics are problem-solving approaches that:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Find good (not necessarily optimal) solutions efficiently
\item
  Handle complex, large-scale, or NP-hard problems
\item
  Use rules of thumb, intuition, or iterative improvement
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{When to Use Heuristics}\label{when-to-use-heuristics}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{NP-hard problems}: Exact solutions computationally infeasible
\item
  \textbf{Large-scale problems}: Solution space too large for exhaustive
  search
\item
  \textbf{Real-time decisions}: Need quick, good-enough solutions
\item
  \textbf{Complex constraints}: Difficult to model mathematically
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Types of Heuristics}\label{types-of-heuristics}

\textbf{Constructive Heuristics:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Build solution from scratch
\item
  Example: Nearest Neighbor for TSP
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Improvement Heuristics:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Start with initial solution and improve it
\item
  Example: Local Search, Simulated Annealing
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Metaheuristics:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  High-level strategies to guide search
\item
  Examples: Genetic Algorithms, Tabu Search
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Greedy Algorithms}\label{greedy-algorithms}

A greedy algorithm makes the best choice \emph{right now}, without
worrying about the future. \emph{Example:} Assign the largest class to
the first available large room. Then the next. Then the next.

\subsubsection{Local Search Algorithms}\label{local-search-algorithms}

Imagine you are blindfolded on a mountain and want to reach the peak.
You step in a random direction. If you go up, you stay there. If you go
down, you step back. You keep doing this until you can't go up anymore.

\textbf{Basic Local Search:}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Start with initial solution
\item
  Evaluate neighborhood (solutions reachable by small change)
\item
  Move to best neighbor
\item
  Repeat until no improving neighbor exists
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Variants:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Hill Climbing}: Always move to better solution
\item
  \textbf{Steepest Ascent}: Evaluate all neighbors, choose best
\item
  \textbf{Random Restart}: Multiple runs from different starting points
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Applications in Campus
Planning}\label{applications-in-campus-planning}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Room Assignment}: Assign classes to rooms heuristically
\item
  \textbf{Exam Scheduling}: Create conflict-free exam schedules
\item
  \textbf{Maintenance Routing}: Plan efficient maintenance routes
\item
  \textbf{Resource Allocation}: Allocate limited resources optimally
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Sample Problem}\label{sample-problem-12}

\textbf{Problem Statement:} Schedule 5 campus events into 3 time slots
(Morning, Afternoon, Evening) with preferences:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Events A, B conflict (can't be same slot)
\item
  Event C prefers Morning
\item
  Event D prefers Afternoon or Evening
\item
  Event E has no preference Use greedy heuristic to create schedule.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Heuristic Solution:}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Place C in Morning (highest priority preference)
\item
  Place A in Afternoon (avoids conflict with C)
\item
  Place B in Evening (avoids conflict with A)
\item
  Place D in Afternoon (preference satisfied)
\item
  Place E in remaining slot (Evening)
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Result:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Morning: C
\item
  Afternoon: A, D
\item
  Evening: B, E
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Sample Problem}\label{sample-problem-13}

\textbf{Problem Statement:} Optimize delivery routes for campus catering
using 2-opt local search heuristic for TSP.

\textbf{Initial Route:} Warehouse → A → B → C → D → E → Warehouse
(Distance: 45 km)

\textbf{2-opt Operation:} Try swapping pairs of edges to reduce total
distance.

\textbf{Example Improvement:}

Swap B-C and D-E connections: New route: Warehouse → A → B → D → C → E →
Warehouse If distance \textless{} 45 km, accept the swap.

\subsection{Review Questions}\label{review-questions-10}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Explain the trade-off between solution quality and computational time
  when using heuristic algorithms.
\item
  What is the ``local optimum'' problem in local search algorithms and
  how can it be addressed?
\item
  Design a greedy heuristic for assigning project teams to campus
  facilities based on proximity and capacity.
\item
  Compare constructive heuristics vs improvement heuristics. When would
  you use each approach?
\item
  How can heuristics be combined with exact methods (like CPM) for
  complex campus planning problems?
\end{enumerate}

\section{Micro-Project 3: Campus Construction
Planning}\label{micro-project-3-campus-construction-planning}

\subsection{Project Overview}\label{project-overview}

\subsubsection{Business Context}\label{business-context}

Campus City is planning a major expansion: construction of a new Student
Center. As the project planning specialist, you are responsible for
developing the complete project plan using CPM, PERT, and heuristic
optimization techniques.

\subsubsection{Project Scope}\label{project-scope}

The new Student Center construction involves:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Site preparation and utilities
\item
  Main structure construction
\item
  Interior finishing and amenities
\item
  External landscaping
\item
  Final commissioning and handover
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Project Data
Specification}\label{project-data-specification}

\subsubsection{Activities and
Dependencies}\label{activities-and-dependencies}

Based on campus construction standards and previous projects:

\textbf{Phase 1: Site Preparation (Weeks 1-8)}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  A: Clear site (3 weeks)
\item
  B: Excavate foundation (2 weeks, after A)
\item
  C: Install utilities (4 weeks, after A)
\item
  D: Pour foundation (3 weeks, after B, C)
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Phase 2: Structure Construction (Weeks 9-20)}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{4}
\tightlist
\item
  E: Erect steel frame (6 weeks, after D)
\item
  F: Install roofing (3 weeks, after E)
\item
  G: External walls (4 weeks, after E)
\item
  H: Windows/doors (2 weeks, after F, G)
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Phase 3: Interior Work (Weeks 21-32)}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{8}
\tightlist
\item
  I: Electrical systems (5 weeks, after H)
\item
  J: Plumbing systems (4 weeks, after H)
\item
  K: HVAC installation (4 weeks, after H)
\item
  L: Drywall/painting (3 weeks, after I, J, K)
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Phase 4: Finishing (Weeks 33-40)}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{12}
\tightlist
\item
  M: Flooring (3 weeks, after L)
\item
  N: Fixtures/furniture (2 weeks, after M)
\item
  O: Landscaping (4 weeks, after G)
\item
  P: Final inspection (1 week, after N, O)
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Probabilistic Time
Estimates}\label{probabilistic-time-estimates}

For critical activities, provide three-point estimates:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  E: Steel erection: 5, 6, 8 weeks
\item
  I: Electrical: 4, 5, 7 weeks
\item
  M: Flooring: 2, 3, 5 weeks
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Resource Constraints}\label{resource-constraints}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Maximum 3 construction crews available
\item
  Specialized equipment available for 2 activities simultaneously
\item
  Budget constraint: \$5,000,000 total
\item
  Crew costs: \$10,000/week per crew
\end{itemize}

\chapter{Module 4: Combinatorial Optimization and Graph
Algorithms}\label{module-4-combinatorial-optimization-and-graph-algorithms}

\section{Module Overview}\label{module-overview-3}

\subsection{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-4}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Understand fundamental combinatorial optimization problems and their
  applications
\item
  Master graph algorithms for network optimization (Prim's, Kruskal's,
  Dijkstra's)
\item
  Solve classic problems: Traveling Salesman, Transportation, and
  Assignment
\item
  Apply graph algorithms to real-world campus logistics challenges
\item
  Develop algorithmic thinking for complex optimization scenarios
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Module Structure}\label{module-structure-2}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Section 4.1}: Introduction to Combinatorial Optimization
\item
  \textbf{Section 4.2}: Graph Algorithms I - Minimum Spanning Trees
\item
  \textbf{Section 4.3}: Graph Algorithms II - Shortest Path Problems
\item
  \textbf{Section 4.4}: Classic Combinatorial Problems
\item
  \textbf{Section 4.5}: Advanced Applications \& Micro-Project 4
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Real-World Context}\label{real-world-context-2}

Apply combinatorial optimization to design efficient campus
transportation networks, optimize delivery routes, and solve resource
allocation problems using graph theory.

\section{Introduction to Combinatorial
Optimization}\label{introduction-to-combinatorial-optimization}

In the previous modules, we dealt with continuous numbers---smooth
curves, gradients, and decimal values. We could tune a variable from 1.0
to 1.1.

But the world of Computer Science is rarely smooth. It is
\textbf{Discrete}.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  You cannot send 1.5 data packets.
\item
  You cannot visit 3.4 cities.
\item
  You are either ``connected'' or ``disconnected.''
\end{itemize}

This brings us to \textbf{Combinatorial Optimization}: the mathematics
of making the best choice when the options are distinct, separate, and
usually countable. This is the engine behind Google Maps, DNA
sequencing, and Amazon's delivery network.

\subsection{Theoretical Foundation}\label{theoretical-foundation-8}

\subsubsection{What is Combinatorial
Optimization?}\label{what-is-combinatorial-optimization}

Imagine you are at a restaurant.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Continuous Optimization} is like determining exactly how much
  salt to put in a soup. You can add 1 gram, 1.1 grams, or 1.005 grams.
  You slide along a smooth scale until it tastes perfect.
\item
  \textbf{Combinatorial Optimization} is like choosing items from the
  menu to stay within a budget while maximizing calories. You cannot
  order ``half a burger'' or ``root of a pizza.'' You must choose
  distinct items.
\end{itemize}

Combinatorial optimization deals with finding optimal solutions from a
finite set of possibilities. Unlike continuous optimization, we work
with discrete structures like graphs, networks, and permutations.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, arc=.35mm, opacityback=0, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Formal Definition:}, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, coltitle=black, breakable, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

Combinatorial optimization is the process of finding an optimal object
from a finite set of objects. In most such problems, the set of feasible
solutions is discrete or can be reduced to a discrete set.

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsubsection{Key Characteristics}\label{key-characteristics}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Discrete Decision Spaces}: Solutions involve discrete choices
  (yes/no, sequences, selections)
\item
  \textbf{Exponential Complexity}: Many problems have solution spaces
  that grow exponentially
\item
  \textbf{NP(Nondeterministic Polynomial)-Hard Problems}: Many
  real-world problems are computationally challenging
\item
  \textbf{Heuristic Approaches}: Often necessary for large-scale
  problems
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Mathematical
Representation}\label{mathematical-representation-1}

A typical combinatorial optimization problem:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Minimize } & f(x) \\
\text{Subject to } & x \in S \\
\end{aligned}
\] Where:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(x\): Vector of discrete decisions
\item
  \(S\): Finite set of feasible solutions
\item
  \(f(x)\): Objective function to minimize
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Campus City
Applications}\label{campus-city-applications-1}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Facility Network Design}: Connecting campus buildings
  optimally
\item
  \textbf{Delivery Route Planning}: Finding shortest paths for supply
  distribution
\item
  \textbf{Resource Allocation}: Assigning resources to facilities
  efficiently
\item
  \textbf{Scheduling Problems}: Timetabling and sequencing operations
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Why is this hard? (The Combinatorial
Explosion)}\label{why-is-this-hard-the-combinatorial-explosion}

You might think, \emph{``If the set is finite, why don't we just check
every possibility?''}

This is the trap. Let's look at the \textbf{Traveling Salesperson
Problem (TSP)}: visiting \(N\) cities exactly once and returning home.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{3 Cities:} 1 possible route. Easy.
\item
  \textbf{5 Cities:} 12 routes. easy.
\item
  \textbf{10 Cities:} 181,440 routes. Doable.
\item
  \textbf{20 Cities:} \(6 \times 10^{16}\) routes. A supercomputer would
  take centuries.
\end{itemize}

In CS terms, the search space grows \textbf{factorially} (\(n!\)). This
is why we need smart math---we cannot just ``brute force'' our way
through.

\subsection{Sample Problem}\label{sample-problem-14}

\textbf{Problem Statement:}

Campus City needs to connect 5 academic buildings with fiber optic
cables. The connections form a complete graph with the following
distances (in meters):

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llllll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
From ~To & A & B & C & D & E \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
A & - & 300 & 400 & 500 & 200 \\
B & 300 & - & 350 & 450 & 250 \\
C & 400 & 350 & - & 300 & 400 \\
D & 500 & 450 & 300 & - & 350 \\
E & 200 & 250 & 400 & 350 & - \\
\end{longtable}

Find the minimum total cable length to connect all buildings (not
necessarily directly).

\textbf{Solution Approach:}

This is a Minimum Spanning Tree problem. We'll solve it in Section 4.2
using Prim's or Kruskal's algorithm.

\textbf{Key Insight:} We need to connect all nodes with minimum total
edge weight, not necessarily creating direct connections between every
pair.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
